--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the foraging mission carefully.

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering the "good" item (one color side), increases by 1 for "bad" item
- We don't know which side (BLUE or GREEN) is good vs bad initially
- Robots need to: explore → find source → pick up correct item → return to nest (WHITE floor, near RED wall)
- Key perceptions: floor color (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=transit), wall colors (RED=nest side, BLUE/GREEN=source sides)

**Strategy:**
- State 0: Explore/move toward RED wall (nest area indicator) when carrying good item, or explore source
- Better: Go toward BLACK floor to collect, then go toward WHITE floor to deliver
- Need to pick one color side (say BLUE=good, or GREEN=good - unknown, so try both)

**FSM Designs:**

1. **Simple 2-state**: Explore → GoToColor(BLACK) → when on BLACK go to WHITE side
2. **3-state**: Explore → GoToBlack (source) → GoToRed wall (nest return)
3. **4-state**: Full pipeline with color discrimination

The key insight: GoToColor with color=1(red wall) takes robots to nest. GoToColor with color=2(green) or color=3(blue) takes to source sides. Floor BLACK triggers return.

Colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify based on my Round 2 designs.

**Key reasoning:**
- The mission rewards delivering the "correct" item (decreases score by 1) and penalizes wrong item delivery (+1)
- Robots should: go to source → pick up item from the correct color side → return to nest (WHITE floor, RED wall)
- The correct side is unknown: need to try both BLUE (clr=3) and GREEN (clr=2) as targets
- Return signal: WHITE floor = nest area; RED wall (clr=1) = nest direction
- Critical: robots should avoid the wrong source half, or at minimum deliver items consistently from one side

**Strategy refinements:**
- Add explicit "go away from wrong color" states
- Use floor-color transitions more aggressively (BLACK→return, WHITE→delivered→go back)
- Try pure GoToColor pipelines without exploration states
- Mix neighbor conditions to handle congestion

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received any score feedback. I need to reason carefully about what should work best and diversify my approach.

**Key insight I may have been missing:**
- The robot picks up an item just by *entering* a source half - it can't choose not to pick up
- If it enters the wrong half, it gets a bad item (score +1)
- The robot should actively AVOID the wrong color side and SEEK the correct color side
- After getting an item (on BLACK floor), it should return to nest (WHITE floor / RED wall)
- The GoAwayColor behavior (B=9) can steer away from the wrong side wall

**Critical realization:** When on BLACK floor, the robot already HAS an item. It should immediately head to the nest (GoToColor RED wall). The challenge is reaching the correct source half first.

**Revised core loop:**
- State A: GoToColor(BLUE or GREEN) - approach correct source
- State B: When on BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED) - return to nest  
- State C (optional): When on WHITE floor → go back to source

The transition from BLACK floor should be very reliable (probability ~1.0).

I still have no score feedback being shown to me. I need to reason more carefully and try more diverse approaches.

**Deep analysis:**

The problem is I don't know which color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) gives the good item. Both should be tried equally. But more importantly, I need to think about what actually works physically:

1. GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) steers toward that source wall → robot enters that half → gets that item
2. On BLACK floor → robot has item → GoToColor(RED=1) steers toward nest wall → enters WHITE → delivers
3. On WHITE floor → delivered → go back to source

**Critical issue:** When returning from nest (WHITE floor), the robot might re-enter and deliver nothing, wasting time. Need WHITE→source transition.

**Also:** Maybe the best strategy avoids the wrong color entirely using GoAwayColor of the bad wall while approaching. But we don't know which is bad.

**New idea:** What if robots just do pure exploration and deliver whatever they find? Score might average to 0. We need to specifically target one side.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score data being returned to me. This is a serious problem - I'm designing blind. Let me think much more carefully about the problem structure.

**Fundamental re-analysis:**

The score is MINIMISED. Items from one half decrease score by 1 (good), items from the other increase by 1 (bad). We don't know which is which - BLUE(3) or GREEN(2).

**Key insight I've been missing:** The problem says "an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1." This is fixed per run. So consistently delivering from ONE side is optimal - if it's the right side, you get -N; if wrong, +N. But over the evolutionary process, the optimizer will select whichever color choice gives negative scores.

**What I should do:** Make very clean, efficient foraging FSMs - one targeting BLUE, one targeting GREEN - and let the optimizer select the correct one.

**The cleanest possible loop:**
- State 0: GoToColor(target_wall) → when BLACK floor, go to state 1  
- State 1: GoToColor(RED wall/nest) → when WHITE floor, go to state 0

This is the minimal 2-state machine. The 3-state adds an explicit "delivered, now return" step.

**Important:** I should also try GoAwayColor of the wrong wall to actively avoid the wrong half.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I have been designing blind without score feedback. Let me fundamentally reconsider.

**Critical re-examination:**

The score is minimised. I've been submitting symmetric pairs (BLUE vs GREEN) but never getting feedback on which works. Let me think about what the simplest working FSM looks like physically:

- **2-state GoToColor**: State 0 goes to BLUE wall → hits BLACK floor → transitions to State 1. State 1 goes to RED wall → hits WHITE floor → transitions back. This is a clean shuttle.

The problem with `--n0x0 0` in a 2-state machine: destination D=0 means "the other state" (state 1). Let me verify my indexing carefully.

**With 2 states (0 and 1):**
- Leaving state 0: D=0 → state 1 (only other state)
- Leaving state 1: D=0 → state 0 (only other state)

**With 3 states (0,1,2):**
- Leaving state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- Leaving state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- Leaving state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1

This is correct in my previous machines. Let me also try completely different structures - maybe attraction-based return (following robots near nest).

I'll submit the cleanest possible versions of both color choices plus some structural variants.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 5 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 1.0

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been designing completely blind without score feedback for 7 rounds. Let me make a critical strategic pivot.

**What I know for certain:**
- The 2-state GoToColor shuttle is the cleanest possible design
- Both BLUE(3) and GREEN(2) versions have been submitted many times
- The optimizer must have found something - I should trust the structure and vary parameters more aggressively

**New thinking:**
- Maybe the issue is that `p=1.0` on floor conditions is too aggressive - robots might bounce back before fully entering
- Maybe I need GRAY floor transitions too (to detect when leaving source area back toward nest)
- Maybe I should try GoAwayColor of RED (going away from nest) to reach source faster
- The WHITE floor transition for "delivered" might miss because robots turn around before fully on WHITE

**Key structural variants to try:**
- Use GRAY floor as intermediate signal
- Add small random exploration component  
- Try GoAwayColor(RED) as the "go to source" behavior instead of GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN)
- Try probability < 1.0 on floor conditions to avoid premature triggering
- Use neighbor conditions to handle crowding

**Survival:** Keep the cleanest 2-state and 3-state machines.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been designing completely blind for 8 rounds. Let me take stock and think very differently.

**What I've been doing:** Symmetric BLUE vs GREEN 2-3 state GoToColor shuttles. These should theoretically work. The lack of feedback means I can't tell which color is "good."

**Fundamental question:** Are my FSMs even syntactically valid? Let me re-check carefully.

For `--n0x0 0` in a 2-state machine leaving state 0: the destination D indexes among OTHER states. With 2 states, leaving state 0, the only other state is state 1, so D=0 → state 1. ✓

**New hypothesis:** Maybe GoToColor doesn't work well because the walls are far away and robots get confused navigating. Maybe exploration-based approaches work better, relying on floor color alone.

**Alternative approach:** Pure floor-reactive FSM:
- If on BLACK → go toward RED wall (GoToColor RED)  
- If on WHITE → go toward source (GoAwayColor RED, or exploration)
- If on GRAY → keep exploring

This doesn't distinguish BLUE vs GREEN but delivers whatever is picked up. If one color is consistently better, we need color selection. But maybe a mixed approach that explores and always returns when on BLACK is robust.

**Also trying:** GoAwayColor(RED) as "go to source" - moves away from nest toward source end.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
This is the final round. I've been designing completely blind for 9 rounds with no score feedback. Let me make my most carefully reasoned final submission.

**Final analysis:**

The core loop must be:
1. Go to source (BLACK floor) → get item
2. Return to nest (WHITE floor) → deliver item

The color choice (BLUE=3 vs GREEN=2) determines whether score goes up or down. Since I've submitted both equally, the optimizer should have found which is better. For this final round I'll submit both but also try completely different structures.

**Key variants I haven't tried enough:**
- GoAwayColor(RED) to navigate to source (moves away from nest wall)
- Pure exploration + floor reactions (no color targeting)
- Mixing GoAwayColor(wrong_color) with GoToColor(right_color) for precise half-selection
- Using GRAY floor condition to detect transit zone

**Most important:** Submit the absolute cleanest versions of each approach, maximizing the chance one works perfectly.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
